Then: (c1) there is exactly one inner product on 𝒜_n for which the inner product of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and *l*₁∧⋯∧*l*_n is the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩, and it is positive definite; (c2) the squared norm of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n is at most *n*! ‖*h*₁‖²⋯‖*h*_n‖²; (c3) for *h* in 𝒦 and for a bounded operator *T* on 𝒦, the rules *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n → *h*∧*h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n, *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n → the sum over *j* of ±⟨*h*, *h*_j⟩ times the product with *h*_j left out (the sign + for odd *j*, − for even *j*), and *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n → *Th*₁∧⋯∧*Th*_n define linear maps *B*†(*h*) from 𝒜_n to 𝒜_(n+1), *B*(*h*) from 𝒜_n to 𝒜_(n−1), and a linear map of 𝒜_n to itself. The antisymmetric Fock space over 𝒦 is the space of sequences (Θ₀, Θ₁, Θ₂, …), with Θ₀ a complex number, Θ_n in the completion of 𝒜_n, and the sum of the ‖Θ_n‖² finite.

*Proof.* (a) Let *F* be the function equal to 1 on a box, the product of intervals from *α*_j to *β*_j, and 0 elsewhere. Then *G*(*ξ*) is the product over *j* of the integrals of exp(−i*ξ*_j*y*) from *α*_j to *β*_j. Each of these has modulus at most *β*_j − *α*_j, and for *ξ*_j ≠ 0 it equals (exp(−i*ξ*_j*α*_j) − exp(−i*ξ*_j*β*_j))/(i*ξ*_j), of modulus at most 2/|*ξ*_j|. Some component has |*ξ*_j| ≥ |*ξ*|/*N*^(1/2), so |*G*(*ξ*)| ≤ *C*/|*ξ*| with *C* depending on the box, and the statement holds for every finite combination of such functions. For a general integrable *F* and *ε* > 0 there is, by the construction of the integral, such a combination *F*₁ with ∫ |*F* − *F*₁| *d*^N*y* ≤ *ε*. Since |exp(−i*ξ*·*y*)| = 1, the functions *G* and *G*₁ differ by at most *ε* at every *ξ*, and *G*₁(*ξ*) → 0; so |*G*(*ξ*)| ≤ 2*ε* for all large |*ξ*|.

(b) Let *x*₀ be a point of the interval and *Q* the open disc about *x*₀ of a radius *r* so small that the real points of the closed disc lie in the interval. On the closed disc put *H*(*z*) = *h*(*z*) for Im *z* ≥ 0 and *H*(*z*) = *h*(*z*∗)∗ for the other *z*. The two expressions agree on the real points, where *h* = 0, so *H* is continuous. *H* is analytic at the points of *Q* above the real axis, and at those below it, where its difference quotient at *z* is the complex conjugate of the difference quotient of *h* at *z*∗. Fix *z* in *Q* with Im *z* > 0, and for small *ε* > 0 let *Q*₁ be the part of the closed disc with Im *w* ≥ *ε* and *Q*₂ the part with Im *w* ≤ −*ε*. On *Q*₁, *H* is the function *h*(*w*), analytic in the open half plane Im *w* > 0, which contains *Q*₁; on *Q*₂, *H* is the function *h*(*w*∗)∗, analytic in the open half plane below the real axis, which contains *Q*₂. By the integral formula of Cauchy, 2πi*H*(*z*) is the integral of *H*(*w*)/(*w* − *z*) around the boundary of *Q*₁; by the theorem of Cauchy, the integral of the same function around the boundary of *Q*₂ is 0. As *ε* → 0 the two integrals along the chords at the heights *ε* and −*ε* tend, by the uniform continuity of *H* on the closed disc, to the integrals along the real diameter in the two opposite directions, whose sum is 0, and the two arcs tend to the whole circle. Hence 2πi*H*(*z*) = ∮ *H*(*w*)/(*w* − *z*) *dw* over the circle of radius *r*. The same holds for Im *z* below 0 by the same argument, and for real *z* in *Q* because both sides are continuous in *z*. The right side may be differentiated with respect to *z* under the integral sign, so *H* is analytic in *Q*. *H* vanishes on the real diameter, so all its derivatives at *x*₀, computed along the real axis, vanish; its power series about *x*₀ is 0, and *H* = 0 in *Q*. Thus *h* vanishes on an open subset of the half plane Im *z* > 0, which is connected; by the identity theorem for analytic functions *h* = 0 there, and by continuity for Im *z* ≥ 0.

(c) Take finitely many products, and let *e*₁, …, *e*_r be an orthonormal basis of the span of all the vectors that occur in them. For a set *I* of *n* of the indices 1, …, *r* and a function *D* of 𝒜_n write *D*(*I*) for the value of *D* at the vectors *e*_i, *i* in *I*, taken in increasing order. Let Π = *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and Π′ = *l*₁∧⋯∧*l*_n be two of the products, and put *c*(*m*, *i*) = ⟨*e*_m, *h*_i⟩, *d*(*m*, *j*) = ⟨*e*_m, *l*_j⟩; then ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩ is the sum over *m* of *c*(*m*, *i*)∗*d*(*m*, *j*). Expanding the determinant as the sum over the permutations *σ* of sgn(*σ*) times the product over *i* of ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩ with *j* = *σ*(*i*), and then each factor as the sum over *m*, gives the sum, over all sequences *m*(1), …, *m*(*n*) of indices, of the product over *i* of *c*(*m*(*i*), *i*)∗ times the determinant of the numbers *d*(*m*(*i*), *j*). This determinant is 0 when two of the *m*(*i*) are equal. The sequences without repetition are the rearrangements of the sets *I*: if *m* is the rearrangement of *I* by a permutation *ρ*, the determinant is sgn(*ρ*) times the square root of *n*! times Π′(*I*), and the sum over *ρ* of sgn(*ρ*) times the product of the *c*(*m*(*i*), *i*)∗ is the square root of *n*! times Π(*I*)∗. Therefore the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *l*_j⟩ equals *n*! times the sum over *I* of Π(*I*)∗Π′(*I*). It follows that for two finite combinations *D* and *D*′ of products, the corresponding combination of determinants equals *n*! times the sum over *I* of *D*(*I*)∗*D*′(*I*). This number depends on the functions *D* and *D*′ alone, which gives the inner product of (c1) and its uniqueness; for *D*′ = *D* it is a sum of squared moduli. If it is 0, then *D*(*I*) = 0 for every *I*; *D* is conjugate-linear in each argument and changes sign when two arguments are exchanged, so *D* vanishes whenever its arguments lie in the span of the *e*_i; and *D* is unchanged when each argument is replaced by its orthogonal projection on that span, since ⟨*g*, *h*⟩ is unchanged for *h* in the span. So *D* = 0. (c2) The determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *h*_j⟩ is a sum of *n*! terms, each a product of *n* inner products in which every *h*_j occurs once on the left and once on the right; by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality each term has modulus at most ‖*h*₁‖²⋯‖*h*_n‖². (c3) Expanding the determinant of *h*∧*h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n along the column of *h* shows that its value at *g*₀, …, *g*_n is (*n* + 1)^(−1/2) times the sum over *i* of ±⟨*g*_i, *h*⟩ times the value of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n at the *g* with *g*_i left out, the sign being + for even *i* and − for odd *i*. Expanding the determinant of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n at the arguments *h*, *g*₁, …, *g*_(n−1) along the row of *h* shows that *n*^(1/2) times this value is the value at *g*₁, …, *g*_(n−1) of the sum stated in (c3). The value of *Th*₁∧⋯∧*Th*_n at *g*₁, …, *g*_n is the value of *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n at *T*†*g*₁, …, *T*†*g*_n. In each of the three cases the image is expressed through the function *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n alone, by a linear rule; so the rule extends to a linear map on combinations. □

Lemma 5.8 uses, without proof, the theorem of Cauchy, the integral formula of Cauchy and the identity theorem of the theory of analytic functions of one variable, and, from the theory of the integral, the approximation of integrable functions by combinations of functions of boxes and the theorem of dominated convergence.

**Corollary 5.9 (Cluster decomposition and the Reeh–Schlieder property for the free field of spin ½).** Let ℱ₋, *V* and Ψ(*f*) be as in Theorem 5.7. (i) For a spacelike vector *a* and all Θ, Θ′ in ℱ₋, ⟨Θ, *V*(*λa*, 1)Θ′⟩ tends to ⟨Θ, Ω⟩⟨Ω, Θ′⟩ as *λ* → ∞. (ii) Let *X* and *Y* be products of operators Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)†, and let *Y*_λ be *Y* with every function *f*(*x*) replaced by *f*(*x* − *λa*). Then ⟨Ω, *XY*_λΩ⟩ tends to ⟨Ω, *X*Ω⟩⟨Ω, *Y*Ω⟩ as *λ* → ∞. (iii) Let *D* be a non-empty open region of spacetime. The vectors obtained from Ω by products of operators Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)†, with every *f* a smooth function vanishing outside a bounded subset of *D*, span a dense subspace of ℱ₋.

*Proof.* (i) As in Theorem 5.5(i), with a matrix *A* of SL(2, ℂ) for which Λ(*A*) is the boost used there (Theorem 6.38), it suffices to treat *a* = (0, **a**) with **a** ≠ 0. Let Θ = *h*₁∧⋯∧*h*_n and Θ′ = *l*₁∧⋯∧*l*_n with *n* ≥ 1. By Lemma 5.8(c), ⟨Θ, *V*(*λa*, 1)Θ′⟩ is the determinant of the numbers ⟨*h*_i, *V*₁*l*_j⟩, where *V*₁ is the action of (*λa*, 1) on 𝒦. Write *h*′ and *h*″ for the parts of a vector *h* of 𝒦 in 𝒦₊ and 𝒦₋. By Theorem 5.7(iv), ⟨*h*, *V*₁*l*⟩ is the sum of ∫ *h*′†*γ*⁰*l*′ exp(−i*λ***a**·**k**) *dμ* and of the complex conjugate of −∫ *h*″†*γ*⁰*l*″ exp(i*λ***a**·**k**) *dμ*. By Theorem 5.7(iii) the form *u*†*γ*⁰*w* is positive definite on the columns with *P*₊(*k*)*u* = *u* and negative definite on those with *P*₋(*k*)*u* = *u*, so the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality holds for it at each *k*, and again for the integrals; the two integrands without the exponentials are therefore integrable functions of **k**. By Lemma 5.8(a) both integrals tend to 0. Every entry of the determinant tends to 0, so the determinant does. Products of different lengths are orthogonal, and for *n* = 0 the inner product is Θ₀∗Θ′₀ = ⟨Θ, Ω⟩⟨Ω, Θ′⟩. So (i) holds for finite combinations of products. For general Θ, Θ′ and combinations Θ₁, Θ₁′, the difference of ⟨Θ, *V*Θ′⟩ and ⟨Θ₁, *V*Θ₁′⟩ is at most ‖Θ − Θ₁‖‖Θ′‖ + ‖Θ₁‖‖Θ′ − Θ₁′‖ for every unitary *V*, and the same bound holds for the limits; the combinations are dense, so (i) holds in general. (ii) By Theorem 5.7(W4) and (W2), *Y*_λΩ = *V*(*λa*, 1)*YV*(*λa*, 1)⁻¹Ω = *V*(*λa*, 1)*Y*Ω, and ⟨Ω, *XY*_λΩ⟩ = ⟨*X*†Ω, *V*(*λa*, 1)*Y*Ω⟩, the operators being bounded. By (i) this tends to ⟨*X*†Ω, Ω⟩⟨Ω, *Y*Ω⟩.

(iii) Let Θ be orthogonal to all the vectors named; it is to be shown that Θ = 0. Step 1. Let *b* be a vector with *b*⁰ > |**b**| and *ν*(*k*) = *b*⁰*k*⁰ − **b**·**k**, which is positive on the mass shell. For Im *z* ≥ 0 let *W*₁(*z*) be the operator on 𝒦 that multiplies each vector at each *k* by the complex number exp(i*zν*(*k*)), in 𝒦₋ in the sense of its conjugate multiplication. Its modulus is at most 1, so *W*₁(*z*) is bounded by 1, and for real *z* it is the action of the translation by *zb*, because *k*·*b* = −*ν*(*k*). For *g*, *l* in 𝒦 the number ⟨*g*, *W*₁(*z*)*l*⟩ is the integral of exp(i*zν*) against a fixed integrable function; it is continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 by dominated convergence, and analytic for Im *z* > 0, where the derivative may be taken under the integral sign because *ν* exp(−*ν* Im *z*) is bounded. Let *W*(*z*) be the map of Lemma 5.8(c3) for *T* = *W*₁(*z*), and let Θ₂ be a finite combination of products. For a product Θ₁, ⟨Θ₁, *W*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ is a combination of determinants of such numbers, hence continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 and analytic for Im *z* > 0. By Lemma 5.8(c2), ‖*W*(*z*)Θ₂‖ is bounded by a constant not depending on *z*. Hence for every Θ₁ in ℱ₋ the function ⟨Θ₁, *W*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ is a uniform limit of such functions, and so is continuous for Im *z* ≥ 0 and, by the integral formula of Cauchy, analytic for Im *z* > 0. For a product *X* of operators *V*(*ζ*, 1), Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)†, which are bounded, the function *h*(*z*) = ⟨Θ, *XW*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ = ⟨*X*†Θ, *W*(*z*)Θ₂⟩ has these properties. By Lemma 5.8(b), if *h* vanishes on an interval of the real axis it vanishes for all real *z*. Steps 2 and 3 are those of Theorem 5.6, with each Φ(*f*_j) replaced by Ψ(*f*_j) or Ψ(*f*_j)†; the vectors to which Step 1 is applied are finite combinations of products, because the operators *V*, *B* and *B*† map such combinations to such combinations. The result is that Θ is orthogonal to the vectors obtained from Ω by products of Ψ(*f*) and Ψ(*f*)† with every *f* smooth and vanishing outside a bounded set. Step 4. Let *F* be the integral of *f* against exp(−i*k*·*x*). Each matrix *γ*^μ has norm 1, so *K*(*k*) has norm at most 4*k*⁰ and *P*₊(*k*) at most (*μ* + 4*k*⁰)/(2*μ*); since *k*⁰ ≥ *μ*, ‖*E*₊*f*‖² = ∫ *F*†*γ*⁰*P*₊*F* *dμ* is at most 5/(4*μ*) times ∫ |*F*|² *d*³*k*. As in Theorem 5.4(W3), (1 + |**k**|²)|*F*| is at most the integral of |(1 − ∇²)*f*| over spacetime. So ‖*E*₊*f*‖, and in the same way ‖*E*₋*f*‖, is at most a constant times that integral. For a Schwartz function *f* and a smooth function *χ* equal to 1 near the origin and vanishing outside a bounded set, the integral of |(1 − ∇²)(*f* − *f*_R)| with *f*_R(*x*) = *f*(*x*)*χ*(*x*/*R*) tends to 0 as *R* → ∞. By the bound of Theorem 5.7(W3), Ψ(*f*_R) tends to Ψ(*f*) in norm. So Θ is orthogonal to all the vectors of Theorem 5.7(W3), which span a dense subspace, and Θ = 0. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Corollary 5.9.** The proof of (iii) uses the same one fact as Theorem 5.6: the factor exp(i*zν*) is bounded for Im *z* ≥ 0 because *ν* is positive on the mass shell, in 𝒦₊ and in 𝒦₋ alike. In 𝒦₋ this is the statement that the solutions with the phase exp(−i*k*·*x*) enter the field through the operator that adds a particle of the conjugate space, so that every state has the positive frequency of the orientation +i*c* (Theorem 5.7(W6)).

**Lemma 5.10 (The wavefunctions made in one region are dense; two spacelike separated regions with correlated wavefunctions).** (i) Let *D* be a non-empty open region of spacetime. The functions *Ef* of Definition 5.1(f), with *f* smooth and vanishing outside a bounded subset of *D*, are dense in ℋ₁. (ii) Let *D*₁ be the set of the points *x* = (*x*⁰, **x**) with (*x*⁰)² + |**x**|² less than 1, and *D*₂ the set of the points with (*x*⁰)² + |**x** − **q**|² less than 1, where **q** = (4, 0, 0). No point of *D*₁ lies within or on the light cone of any point of *D*₂. There are real smooth functions *φ*, vanishing outside *D*₁, and *ψ*, vanishing outside *D*₂, such that ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ is a real number different from 0 and *Eφ*, *Eψ* are linearly independent.

*Proof.* (i) Let *χ* in ℋ₁ be orthogonal to all these *Ef*, and apply the proof of Theorem 5.6 with *n* = 1, with the region *D*′ of that proof chosen bounded, to the vector Ψ of ℱ whose only component is Ψ₁ = *χ*. The functions of that proof, smooth and vanishing outside translates of *D*′ that lie in *D*, are then among the *f* named in (i). Since Φ(*f*)Ω has the only component *Ef*, the hypothesis of that proof holds for *n* = 1, and its conclusion is that *χ* is orthogonal to *Ef* for every Schwartz function *f*. By the proof of Theorem 5.4(W3) these *Ef* include every smooth function of **k** vanishing outside a bounded set, and such functions are dense in ℋ₁. So *χ* = 0. (ii) For *x* in *D*₁ and *y* in *D*₂, |*x*⁰ − *y*⁰| is less than 2 and |**x** − **y**| is greater than 2, so *η*(*x* − *y*, *x* − *y*) > 0. *E* is linear, and ℋ₁ has more than one dimension. By (i) for *D*₂ there is a complex *f* vanishing outside *D*₂ with *Ef* ≠ 0; its real or its imaginary part is a real *ψ* with *Eψ* ≠ 0. By (i) for *D*₁ there is a complex *f* vanishing outside *D*₁ with ⟨*Ef*, *Eψ*⟩ ≠ 0, and its real or its imaginary part is a real *φ* with ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ ≠ 0. By (i) again, the *Ef* for *D*₁ are not all multiples of *Eψ*, so, taking real and imaginary parts, there is a real *φ*₁ vanishing outside *D*₁ with *Eφ*₁ not a multiple of *Eψ*. If *Eφ* is a multiple of *Eψ*, replace *φ* by *φ* + *tφ*₁ with a real *t* ≠ 0 so small that the inner product with *Eψ* remains different from 0. Finally, for real *φ* and *ψ* the number *K*(*φ*, *ψ*) of the proof of Theorem 5.4(W5) is ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ − ⟨*Eψ*, *Eφ*⟩, and it is 0 by that theorem; so ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ equals its complex conjugate. □

**Theorem 5.11 (The connection between spin and statistics for the free fields of spin 0 and spin ½).** Let *φ*, *ψ* be the functions of Lemma 5.10(ii). (a) Spin 0 with the exclusion rule. Let ℱ₋ be the antisymmetric Fock space over ℋ₁ (Lemma 5.8(c)), with the operators *B*, *B*† of Theorem 5.7(v), and let Φ_a(*f*) = *B*(*E*(*f*∗)) + *B*†(*Ef*). Then Φ_a(*f*) is bounded, its adjoint is Φ_a(*f*∗), Φ_a((□ − *μ*²)*f*) = 0, and *V*(*a*, Λ)Φ_a(*f*)*V*(*a*, Λ)⁻¹ = Φ_a(*f*′) with *f*′ as in Theorem 5.4(W4) and with *V* acting on ℱ₋ through *V*₁ on each factor. For all *f*, *g*, Φ_a(*f*)Φ_a(*g*) + Φ_a(*g*)Φ_a(*f*) is the number *J*(*f*, *g*) = ⟨*E*(*f*∗), *Eg*⟩ + ⟨*E*(*g*∗), *Ef*⟩. For the spacelike separated *φ*, *ψ*: *J*(*φ*, *ψ*) = 2⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ ≠ 0, and (Φ_a(*φ*)Φ_a(*ψ*) − Φ_a(*ψ*)Φ_a(*φ*))Ω = 2 *Eφ*∧*Eψ* ≠ 0. The field Φ_a neither anticommutes nor commutes at spacelike separation. (b) Spin ½ with the Bose rule. Let 𝒦 = 𝒦₊ ⊕ 𝒦₋, *E*₊, *E*₋ be as in Theorem 5.7. Let ℱ₁ be any Hilbert space with a unit vector Ω and, for every *h* in 𝒦, operators *A*(*h*), conjugate-linear in *h*, and *A*†(*h*), linear in *h*, which map a dense subspace containing Ω into itself and satisfy there ⟨Θ, *A*(*h*)Θ′⟩ = ⟨*A*†(*h*)Θ, Θ′⟩, *A*(*h*)Ω = 0, *A*(*h*)*A*†(*l*) − *A*†(*l*)*A*(*h*) = ⟨*h*, *l*⟩ and *A*(*h*)*A*(*l*) = *A*(*l*)*A*(*h*). Let Ψ_s(*f*) = *A*(*E*₊*f*) + *A*†(*E*₋*f*), with the adjoint Ψ_s(*f*)† = *A*†(*E*₊*f*) + *A*(*E*₋*f*). Let *e* be a column with *γ*⁰*e* = *e* and *e*†*e* = 1, and *f* = *φe*, *g* = *ψe*. Then Ψ_s(*f*)Ψ_s(*g*)† − Ψ_s(*g*)†Ψ_s(*f*) is the number

⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ + (i/*μ*) ∫ *k*⁰ Im((*Eφ*)∗(*Eψ*)) *dμ*(*k*),

whose real part ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ is different from 0, and (Ψ_s(*f*)Ψ_s(*g*)† + Ψ_s(*g*)†Ψ_s(*f*))Ω ≠ 0. The field Ψ_s neither commutes nor anticommutes at spacelike separation. (c) Summary. Of the two rules of exchange, the free scalar field built on the positive-frequency wavefunctions has the property (W5) with the Bose rule alone (Theorem 5.4), and the free field of spin ½ built on them has it with the exclusion rule alone (Theorem 5.7).

*Proof.* (a) The operators *B*(*h*) and *B*†(*h*) are bounded and adjoint to each other by Theorem 5.7(v), whose proof holds for every Hilbert space 𝒦; so Φ_a(*f*) is bounded with the adjoint *B*†(*E*(*f*∗)) + *B*(*Ef*) = Φ_a(*f*∗). *E*((□ − *μ*²)*f*) = 0 by Theorem 5.4(W3), for *f* and for *f*∗. A unitary operator of ℋ₁ acting on each factor carries *B*(*h*) to *B*(*V*₁*h*) and *B*†(*h*) to *B*†(*V*₁*h*), and *Ef*′ = *V*₁(*a*, Λ)*Ef* by Theorem 5.4(W4). By the relations of Theorem 5.7(v), in Φ_a(*f*)Φ_a(*g*) + Φ_a(*g*)Φ_a(*f*) the two operators *B* give 0, the two operators *B*† give 0, and the mixed terms give ⟨*E*(*f*∗), *Eg*⟩ + ⟨*E*(*g*∗), *Ef*⟩. For real *φ*, *ψ* this is ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ + ⟨*Eψ*, *Eφ*⟩ = 2⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ by Lemma 5.10(ii). Next, Φ_a(*ψ*)Ω = *Eψ*, a product of length 1, and Φ_a(*φ*)*Eψ* = ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩Ω + *Eφ*∧*Eψ*. Exchanging *φ* and *ψ* and subtracting, the multiples of Ω cancel, ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ being real, and *Eφ*∧*Eψ* − *Eψ*∧*Eφ* = 2 *Eφ*∧*Eψ*. Its squared norm is 4(‖*Eφ*‖²‖*Eψ*‖² − |⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩|²), which is positive for linearly independent vectors.

(b) Since the operators *A* commute, so do their adjoints *A*†. Hence the commutator is [*A*(*E*₊*f*)*A*†(*E*₊*g*) − *A*†(*E*₊*g*)*A*(*E*₊*f*)] + [*A*†(*E*₋*f*)*A*(*E*₋*g*) − *A*(*E*₋*g*)*A*†(*E*₋*f*)] = ⟨*E*₊*f*, *E*₊*g*⟩ − ⟨*E*₋*g*, *E*₋*f*⟩, the inner products being those of 𝒦₊ and of 𝒦₋. By Lemma 8.7.2(iii) with (*γ*⁰)² = 1, *γ*⁰ is Hermitian, and *γ*^j*γ*⁰ = −*γ*⁰*γ*^j for *j* = 1, 2, 3; so *e*†*γ*^j*e* = *e*†*γ*^j*γ*⁰*e* = −*e*†*γ*⁰*γ*^j*e* = −*e*†*γ*^j*e*, that is, *e*†*γ*^j*e* = 0, and *e*†*K*(*k*)*e* = −*k*⁰. With *P*₊†*γ*⁰*P*₊ = *γ*⁰*P*₊ and the same for *P*₋ (Theorem 5.7(i)), *e*†*γ*⁰*P*₊(*k*)*e* = (*μ* + *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) and *e*†*γ*⁰*P*₋(*k*)*e* = (*μ* − *k*⁰)/(2*μ*). Since *φ* is real, the integral of *φ*(*x*) exp(i*k*·*x*) is (*Eφ*)∗; so *E*₊*f* = (*Eφ*)*P*₊*e* and *E*₋*f* = (*Eφ*)∗*P*₋*e*, and likewise for *g*. Therefore ⟨*E*₊*f*, *E*₊*g*⟩ = ∫ (*Eφ*)∗(*Eψ*)(*μ* + *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) *dμ*, and, the inner product of 𝒦₋ being the conjugate of −∫ *u*†*γ*⁰*w* *dμ*, ⟨*E*₋*g*, *E*₋*f*⟩ = −∫ (*Eφ*)(*Eψ*)∗(*μ* − *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) *dμ*. The commutator is the first minus the second: the terms with *μ* give Re⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩, which is ⟨*Eφ*, *Eψ*⟩ by Lemma 5.10(ii), and the terms with *k*⁰ give the second term of the statement. The integrals converge because *Eφ* and *Eψ* are Schwartz functions. For the last statement, Ψ_s(*g*)†Ω = *A*†(*E*₊*g*)Ω and Ψ_s(*f*)Ω = *A*†(*E*₋*f*)Ω, and applying Ψ_s(*f*) to the first and Ψ_s(*g*)† to the second gives, by the relations, a multiple of Ω plus 2Θ with Θ = *A*†(*E*₊*g*)*A*†(*E*₋*f*)Ω. Write *h* = *E*₊*g* and *l* = *E*₋*f*; they are orthogonal in 𝒦. Then ⟨Ω, Θ⟩ = ⟨*A*(*l*)*A*(*h*)Ω, Ω⟩ = 0, and ‖Θ‖² = ⟨Ω, *A*(*l*)*A*(*h*)*A*†(*h*)*A*†(*l*)Ω⟩ = ‖*h*‖²‖*l*‖², because *A*(*h*)*A*†(*h*) = ‖*h*‖² + *A*†(*h*)*A*(*h*) and *A*(*h*)*A*†(*l*)Ω = ⟨*h*, *l*⟩Ω = 0. Here ‖*h*‖² = ∫ |*Eψ*|²(*μ* + *k*⁰)/(2*μ*) *dμ* and ‖*l*‖² = ∫ |*Eφ*|²(*k*⁰ − *μ*)/(2*μ*) *dμ*; both are positive, because *Eφ* and *Eψ* are continuous and not identically 0, and *k*⁰ exceeds *μ* except at **k** = 0. So Θ ≠ 0, and it is orthogonal to Ω. The supports of *f* and *g* are those of *φ* and *ψ*. (c) is (a) and (b) with Theorems 5.4(W5) and 5.7(W5). □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem 5.11.** Both fields are built on the wavefunctions of positive frequency, the orientation +i*c*. The exchange relations then produce one of two functions of two points: the difference of exp(i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) and exp(−i*k*·(*x* − *y*)) integrated over the mass shell, or their sum. The difference is a solution of the Klein–Gordon equation with vanishing value at equal times, and by Lemma 5.3, the bound *c* on propagation, it vanishes at spacelike separation; the sum does not (Lemma 5.10(ii)). For spin 0 the Bose rule gives the difference. For spin ½ the form *u*†*γ*⁰*w* is negative on the solutions with the phase exp(−i*k*·*x*) (Theorem 5.7(iii)); this sign appears above as the factor (*μ* − *k*⁰)/(2*μ*), and with it the exclusion rule gives the difference and the Bose rule the sum. Scope of the theorem: it is proved for the free fields of mass *m* > 0 and of spin 0 and ½ constructed in Theorems 5.4 and 5.7, by computation of the two exchange functions. Pauli [90] treated free fields of every spin; his abstract concludes “the necessity of Fermi-Dirac statistics for particles with arbitrary half-integral spin” from the positivity of the energy, and Einstein–Bose statistics for integral spin from commutation at spacelike separation. The theorem for arbitrary fields with the six properties, free or interacting, is that of [10]; it is not proved in the present paper.

**Interacting fields: exact statement of what is open.** Theorems 5.4–5.11 concern free fields. For interacting fields in four spacetime dimensions no construction with the six properties is known. The problem statement of the Clay Mathematics Institute [91] asks for a proof that for any compact simple gauge group a non-trivial quantum Yang–Mills theory exists on ℝ⁴ and has a mass gap, existence including axiomatic properties at least as strong as those of Streater and Wightman [10] and of Osterwalder and Schrader; its authors write that there is not yet “a mathematically complete example of a quantum gauge theory in four-dimensional space-time”. The paper [89] lists as open its target (G1), the fields on the Fock space of many particles across the interactions of the Standard Model, and its target (G3), covariance for all the interactions of the Standard Model. The present paper proves the six properties, cluster decomposition, the Reeh–Schlieder property and the connection between spin and statistics for the free fields of spin 0 and ½, and claims nothing for interacting fields.

**Haag–Kastler (1964).** Rudolf Haag and Daniel Kastler abstracted the Wightman framework into the language of local nets of operator algebras. To each open region *O* of Minkowski space is assigned a C*-algebra *A*(*O*) of observables localized in *O*; the assignment satisfies isotony, locality, covariance, and the spectrum condition. This algebraic quantum field theory programme isolates the algebraic structure of relativistic QFT from the choice of representation, but again presupposes Minkowski space as the indexing manifold. The arena remains exogenous.

**Constructive QFT (Glimm–Jaffe, 1968–present).** James Glimm and Arthur Jaffe, with collaborators including Edward Nelson and Konrad Osterwalder, undertook the construction of interacting quantum field theories satisfying the Wightman or Haag–Kastler axioms. They succeeded in low-dimensional models — φ⁴ in two and three spacetime dimensions, the Yukawa model, the sine-Gordon model. The four-dimensional case — the existence of interacting Yang–Mills theory with a mass gap — remains a Clay Millennium Problem, with active work continuing into the 2020s. This programme treats the axiomatization as a target to be matched by construction; it does not attempt to derive the axioms themselves from anything more fundamental.

**Hydrodynamic limits (Deng–Hani–Ma, 2025).** Hilbert himself, in his 1900 lecture, singled out the kinetic theory of gases as a paradigmatic subgoal of the Sixth Problem, writing of “Boltzmann’s work on the principles of mechanics” and the need to derive “the limiting processes … which lead from the atomistic view to the laws of motion of continua.” Over the twentieth century this became known as Hilbert’s sixth problem in the narrow sense: a rigorous derivation of the Euler and Navier–Stokes equations of fluid dynamics from Newton’s laws for individual particles, by way of the Boltzmann equation as the intermediate mesoscopic theory. Partial results were obtained by Lanford (1975) for short timescales in the Boltzmann–Grad limit, by Bardos–Golse–Levermore (1991, 1993) and Saint-Raymond (2009) on the Boltzmann-to-fluid passage, and by Gallagher–Saint-Raymond–Texier (2014) on the analogous problem for the linear Boltzmann equation.

On 3 March 2025, Yu Deng, Zaher Hani, and Xiao Ma posted to the arXiv (2503.01800) a paper titled *Hilbert’s Sixth Problem: Derivation of Fluid Equations via Boltzmann’s Kinetic Theory,* in which they rigorously derive the compressible Euler and incompressible Navier–Stokes–Fourier equations from hard-sphere particle systems undergoing elastic collisions on 2D and 3D tori. The proof closes the Newton-to-Boltzmann passage by extending their earlier work (arXiv:2408.07818) to control the cumulative effect of recollisions over long histories; combined with the Bardos–Golse–Levermore and Saint-Raymond Boltzmann-to-fluid theorems, this yields a single derivation of macroscopic fluid dynamics from microscopic particle mechanics. The authors themselves write, in their abstract, that the work “resolves Hilbert’s sixth problem, as it pertains to the program of deriving the fluid equations from Newton’s laws by way of Boltzmann’s kinetic theory.” This is the strongest contemporary partial answer in the kinetic-theory branch of the Sixth Problem and the most significant advance on this branch since Lanford 1975.

The achievement is, however, a stride *within* a fixed mechanical arena: Newton’s laws, the toroidal Euclidean three-space on which the particles move, and the deterministic Hamiltonian dynamics are taken as inputs. The Deng–Hani–Ma result thus closes one of the cleanest classical subgoals of the Sixth Problem on its kinetic-theory side; by its authors’ explicit qualification (“as it pertains to”), it does not address the broader axiomatization of physics, the generation of spacetime itself, or the unification of relativity and quantum theory. It is the kind of regional rigorous derivation Hilbert had in mind on the classical-statistical-mechanics side, complementary to the arena-generating programme of the present paper. §8.6 returns to this comparison in the context of the McGucken derivation of the second law from the McGucken Operator *D_M* acting on the McGucken Sphere *S_M*(τ).

**Other contemporary programmes.** Causal set theory (Bombelli, Lee, Meyer, Sorkin, 1987) generates Lorentzian spacetime from a discrete partial order, with two primitives (a set and an order relation), but does not unify with quantum theory. Twistor theory (Penrose, 1967) recasts 4-dimensional Lorentzian geometry in terms of complex projective 3-space, but the relationship to quantum amplitudes is a correspondence rather than a derivation. Spectral triples (Connes, 1980s–1990s) encode geometry in a triple (*A*, *H*, *D*) of an algebra, a Hilbert space, and a Dirac operator—three independent inputs, none derived from a single law. Loop quantum gravity, spin foam models, geometric algebra, and the topos-theoretic programme of Döring–Isham each address fragments of the Hilbertian demand without supplying its source-law.

The pattern is consistent across all of these programmes—and across the Deng–Hani–Ma 2025 hydrodynamic-limits breakthrough as well: each axiomatizes or rigorously derives a *region*—probability, non-relativistic QM, relativistic QFT, discrete spacetime, gauge structure, fluid dynamics from Newton—and each takes the arena of that region as primitive. None has supplied the source-law that generates the arena. The McGucken Axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, presented in §4, is the law that fills this gap: it generates the arena and the dynamics from a single line, and the six regional programmes surveyed above appear, retrospectively, as the shadows it casts on probability, on non-relativistic QM, on QFT, on causal sets, on gauge structure, and on the Newton-to-Boltzmann-to-fluid chain. The remainder of this paper makes that statement precise.

## 6. Establishing a New Categorical Foundation for Mathematical Physics which Completes the Erlangen Programme

The McGucken Principle asserts that the fourth dimension *x*₄ is expanding at the velocity of light: *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, with *x*₄ = i*ct*, as a spherically symmetric McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h* and whose volume defines a nonlocality.

## **From Erlangen 1872 to the Source-Law of 2026.**

On 7 October 1872, the twenty-three-year-old Felix Klein delivered his inaugural address at the University of Erlangen, the document now known as the *Vergleichende Betrachtungen über neuere geometrische Forschungen* — the Erlangen Programme. The proposal was radical for its time: every geometry, Klein argued, is the theory of the *invariants* of a transformation group acting on a homogeneous space. Each geometry is therefore captured by a Klein pair (*G*, *H*) — *G* the group of motions, *H* the stabilizer of a chosen point — with the underlying space realized as the coset space *G*/*H*. Euclidean, affine, projective, conformal, hyperbolic, and elliptic geometries, which had grown up as separate disciplines through the nineteenth century, were thereby unified inside a single conceptual frame. The Erlangen Programme was the first time geometry was organized by a meta-principle rather than by a list of axioms about points and lines.

The programme’s influence was, and remains, enormous. Sophus Lie’s continuous groups gave Klein pairs their analytic backbone. Henri Poincaré’s work on automorphic functions and the foundations of relativity adopted Klein’s group-theoretic stance. Élie Cartan in the 1920s generalized homogeneous spaces to *Cartan geometries*, in which the Klein pair becomes the local model of a curved geometry — the language in which general relativity was eventually rewritten. Hermann Weyl’s 1918 and 1929 papers on gauge invariance, Charles Ehresmann’s 1950 theory of fibre bundles and connections, and the entire Yang–Mills programme of the 1950s and beyond all sit inside the Erlangen frame. In a parallel development, Saunders Mac Lane and Samuel Eilenberg in 1945 introduced category theory, and Alexander Grothendieck in the 1960s reorganized algebraic geometry around it; this raised the natural question whether geometry, like algebra, ought to be organized as a category whose objects are spaces and whose morphisms preserve the relevant structure. To that question the Erlangen Programme had no answer of its own.

What Klein’s programme accomplished — and what it never attempted — must be stated cleanly. It accomplished the *classification* of geometries by their symmetry groups. It never attempted to *generate* the Klein pair from a deeper law. The group *G* and the stabilizer *H* are posited externally; the geometry begins once they are chosen. For 154 years no source was offered. Cartan, Weyl, Ehresmann, and Yang–Mills extended the geometric vocabulary that follows from a Klein pair, but they presupposed the pair. Mac Lane, Eilenberg, and Grothendieck supplied the categorical vocabulary, but the spaces and operators inhabiting their categories were still posited, not generated. The Erlangen Programme thus stood as a *frame without a source*, awaiting completion by some law that could simultaneously produce the space, the group, and the operator algebra of physics.

This is the gap that the McGucken Principle closes. The single law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* generates, in one stroke, both members of the Klein pair on which mathematical physics rests. The completion is not metaphorical: there is a theorem that the law produces the symmetry group, and a theorem that the law produces the categorical structure, and these two theorems are independent routes from the same axiom. After 154 years the Erlangen Programme finally has a source.

## **The Source-Pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) and the Category McG**.

The McGucken Principle supplies the missing source explicitly. From the single law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* one obtains *simultaneously* two structures — the McGucken Space *M_G* and the McGucken Operator *D_M* — neither prior to the other, neither posited as a separate axiom, each generating and containing the other in a reciprocal pair [63, 66]. The Theorem of Section 4 establishes this co-generation rigorously: the McGucken Space *M_G* is the integrated kernel of the source law and the McGucken Operator *D_M* is the unique normalized tangent operator to that kernel; the same one-line equation produces both, with no auxiliary postulate.

This co-generation defines a new category — here written **McG** — whose objects are pairs (*X*, *D*) of a space co-generated with its operator algebra by an instance of the source law, and whose morphisms are the maps that preserve the source-pair structure. In its current form [74, Definitions 3.1–3.2] the category is the Six-Object McGucken Category McG₆. Its objects are the six members of the McGucken Source-Tuple, namely the McGucken Sphere, the Moving-Dimension Manifold, the McGucken Space, the McGucken Operator, the McGucken Symmetry and the McGucken Action, together with a distinguished source-axiom point representing *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. Its morphisms are the canonical extractions recovering *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* from each object, the construction rules building each object from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, and the 30 generation procedures between ordered pairs of distinct objects. The category **McG** is not a renaming of an existing category; the source law itself is part of the data of every object, and the morphisms must intertwine the source-law action. In **McG**, Minkowski space with its derivation operator, Hilbert space with its evolution generator, phase space with its Poisson bracket, and gauge bundle with its covariant derivative are *objects*, not separately axiomatized arenas.

## **The Source-Pair Theorems.**

Three theorems establish the category **McG** as a genuine foundation rather than a renaming of standard structure [66]:

**(T1) Mutual Containment.** Every state of the McGucken Space *M_G* determines an action of the McGucken Operator *D_M*, and every action of the McGucken Operator *D_M* realizes a state of the McGucken Space *M_G*. Neither structure is a sub-object of an external ambient category; each is contained in the other through the source law. The theorem is MCC₆, Generalized Mutual Containment (Theorem 4.1 of [74]): for every object *X* of the McGucken Source-Tuple there exists a canonical extraction procedure Π_X : *X* → *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* that recovers the McGucken Axiom in full from *X* alone, using only standard mathematical operations.

**(T2) Reciprocal Generation.** The map *M_G* → *D_M* induced by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is invertible at the level of generators. The space generates the operator and the operator generates the space; there is no asymmetry of priority, and the inversion is not a separate postulate but a consequence of the single law. The theorem is the Reciprocal Generation Theorem (Theorem 3.7 of [74]): the source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) generated by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* satisfies (R1) every point *p* of the McGucken Space generates a McGucken Operator at *p* uniquely; (R2) the family of all pointwise McGucken Operators generates the McGucken Space as a whole, the carrier, the constraint function up to a nonzero scalar, the global operator and the McGucken Sphere wavefront structure each being reconstructed (Operator-to-Space Theorem, Theorem 3.6 of [74]); (R3) the generations R1 and R2 are simultaneous and reciprocal, with no temporal or logical priority of point over operator or operator over point.

**(T3) Containment of the Klein Pair.** The following Klein pairs (*G*, *H*) are proved in this paper to descend from (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*): the pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) of spacetime (Theorems 6.28 and 6.30), the unitary pair of the space of states (Theorem 6.33), the symplectic pair of phase space (Theorem 6.34), the Euclidean pair of a spatial slice (Theorem 6.36), the Galilean pair as the limit of large *c* (Theorem 6.37), the spin pair (Theorem 6.38), the conformal pair of the null cone (Theorems 6.39 and 6.41) and the de Sitter and anti-de Sitter pairs (Theorem 6.43); for a curved spacetime the group of Theorem 6.28 acts at each event (Theorem 6.44). The gauge pairs enter through the covariantization of the McGucken Operator *D_M* (Theorems 8.5.4 and 8.7.8), the selection of the group U(1) × SU(2) × SU(3) being Conjecture 31 of [67] and the subject of [82]. The Erlangen programme is thereby contained in McG, not adjoined to it [63, 66]. For the relativistic pair this is the McGucken Symmetry object of McG₆, the invariance group of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, from which the Klein pair (ISO(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) descends [74, Table 3.1]. The three source-pair theorems are stated and proved in full below as the Mutual Containment Theorem (Theorem 6.16), the Reciprocal Generation Theorem (Theorem 6.20) and the Containment-Generation Equivalence (Theorem 6.23), the descent of the Klein pair of spacetime as Theorems 6.28 and 6.30, and the further pairs as Theorems 6.33, 6.34, 6.36–6.39 and 6.41–6.44.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Source-Pair: Definitions, Lemmas, and the Mutual Containment Theorem, the Reciprocal Generation Theorem and the Containment-Generation Equivalence, with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For the pair formed by the McGucken Space and the McGucken Operator the Principle predicts three facts: each member of the pair holds the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in full, each member produces the other by an explicit procedure, and these two facts are equivalent. The statements and proofs below are those of [66], §§2–5, reproduced in full so that each step can be checked here.

**Definition 6.1 (Four-coordinate carrier; Definition 2.2 of [66]).** The four-coordinate carrier is *E*₄ = {(*x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃, *x*₄) : *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃ ∈ ℝ, *x*₄ ∈ ℂ}, with **x** = (*x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃) the spatial three-vector and *t* ∈ ℝ the temporal parameter against which the advance of the fourth coordinate is measured.

**Definition 6.2 (McGucken constraint and constraint surface; Definition 2.3 of [66]).** The McGucken constraint function is Φ_M(*t*, *x*₄) = *x*₄ − i*ct*. The McGucken constraint surface is the zero set Φ_M = 0, that is, the set of (*t*, *x*₄) ∈ ℝ × ℂ with *x*₄ = i*ct*. It is the surface *C_M* = 0 of §2, since *C_M* = (i/*c*) Φ_M.

**Definition 6.3 (McGucken Operator; Definition 2.4 of [66]).** The McGucken Operator is the first-order linear differential McGucken Operator *D_M* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄, acting on functions *Ψ*(*t*, **x**, *x*₄) [differentiable in *t* and holomorphic in *x*₄] by *D_M* *Ψ* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + i*c* ∂₄*Ψ*.

**Definition 6.4 (The McGucken Sphere; Definition 2.5 of [66]).** To each event *p* of the constraint surface the spherically symmetric expansion of *x*₄ at rate *c* from *p* assigns the McGucken Sphere of *p*: at parameter time *t* it is the 2-sphere of radius *c*(*t* − *t*₀) centered at *p*, where *t*₀ is the time of *p*.

**Definition 6.5 (McGucken Space; Definition 2.6 of [66]).** The McGucken Space *M_G* is the four-tuple formed by the carrier *E*₄, the constraint function Φ_M, the McGucken Operator *D_M*, and the assignment of the McGucken Sphere to every event. The McGucken Space is a space together with its law: the constraint, the flow operator and the spherical propagation are constituent data.

**Definition 6.6 (Source-pair; Definition 2.7 of [66]).** The McGucken source-pair is the ordered pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*).

**Definition 6.7 (Containment, constructive sense; Definition 2.9 of [66]).** An object *X* contains a relation *R* if there is a definite procedure that extracts *R* from *X* explicitly.

**Definition 6.8 (Generation, constructive sense; Definition 2.10 of [66]).** An object *X* generates an object *Y* if there is a definite procedure that produces *Y* from *X* explicitly.

**Lemma 6.9 (Tangency of the McGucken Operator; Lemma 3.1 of [66]).** *D_M* Φ_M = 0.

*Proof.* By Definition 6.2, Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, so ∂ₜΦ_M = −i*c* and ∂₄Φ_M = 1. Therefore *D_M* Φ_M = ∂ₜΦ_M + i*c* ∂₄Φ_M = −i*c* + i*c* = 0. Hence the McGucken Operator *D_M* is tangent to every level set of Φ_M, in particular to the constraint surface. □

**Lemma 6.10 (Characteristic invariance; Lemma 3.2 and Corollary 3.3 of [66]).** For every differentiable function *F* of one complex variable, *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) = *F*(*x*₄ − i*ct*) satisfies *D_M* *Ψ* = 0. Conversely, every *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) [differentiable in *t* and holomorphic in *x*₄] with *D_M* *Ψ* = 0 is a function of *u* = *x*₄ − i*ct* alone.

*Proof.* Let *u* = *x*₄ − i*ct* and *Ψ* = *F*(*u*). The chain rule gives ∂ₜ*Ψ* = −i*c* *F*′(*u*) and ∂₄*Ψ* = *F*′(*u*), so *D_M* *Ψ* = −i*c* *F*′(*u*) + i*c* *F*′(*u*) = 0. Conversely, change variables from (*t*, *x*₄) to (*t*, *u*) and write *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) = *G*(*t*, *u*). Then ∂ₜ*Ψ* = ∂ₜ*G* − i*c* ∂_u*G* and ∂₄*Ψ* = ∂_u*G*, so *D_M* *Ψ* = ∂ₜ*G*. Hence *D_M* *Ψ* = 0 exactly when *G* is independent of *t*, that is, when *Ψ* depends on (*t*, *x*₄) through *u* alone. □

**Lemma 6.11 (Generator equivalence; Lemma 3.4 of [66]).** (a) The integral curves of the McGucken Operator *D_M*, viewed as the vector field (1, i*c*) on the (*t*, *x*₄)-plane, satisfy *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. (b) The chain-rule derivative along any curve satisfying *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* equals the McGucken Operator *D_M* acting on the function being differentiated.

*Proof.* (a) The integral curves of the vector field (1, i*c*) satisfy *dt*/*ds* = 1 and *dx*₄/*ds* = i*c*, where *s* is the curve parameter. Dividing, *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. (b) For *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄) and any curve satisfying *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, *dΨ*/*dt* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + (*dx*₄/*dt*) ∂₄*Ψ* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + i*c* ∂₄*Ψ* = *D_M* *Ψ*. □

**Theorem 6.12 (Containment of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in the McGucken Operator; Theorem 5.1 of [66]).** Let *L* = *a* ∂ₜ + *b* ∂₄ be a first-order linear differential operator on the (*t*, *x*₄)-plane with *a*, *b* ∈ ℂ. The following two conditions together force *L* = *D_M*: (i) *L* is tangent to the constraint surface, *L*Φ_M = 0; (ii) *L* is normalized in the temporal direction, *a* = 1. Under these conditions *b* = i*c*, and the ratio of coefficients *b*/*a* = i*c* recovers the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The law is therefore contained in the McGucken Operator *D_M* as the ratio of its coefficients.

*Proof.* Tangency requires *L*Φ_M = *a* ∂ₜΦ_M + *b* ∂₄Φ_M = −i*ac* + *b* = 0, so *b* = i*ac*. With *a* = 1 this gives *b* = i*c*, hence *L* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ = *D_M*. The ratio *b*/*a* = i*c* is the slope *dx*₄/*dt* of the integral curves of *L* by Lemma 6.11(a). The extraction procedure is: read off the coefficients, take their ratio, and read the result as *dx*₄/*dt*. □

**Theorem 6.13 (Operator-containment of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in the McGucken Space; Theorem 5.3 of [66]).** The law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is contained in the McGucken Space *M_G* through its third component the McGucken Operator *D_M*.

*Proof.* By Definition 6.5, the McGucken Space *M_G* has the McGucken Operator *D_M* as its third component. By Theorem 6.12, the McGucken Operator *D_M* contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* as the ratio of its coefficients. Containment in the sense of Definition 6.7 is transitive: if *X* contains *Y* and *Y* contains *Z*, composing the two extraction procedures extracts *Z* from *X*. The procedure here is: project onto the third component to obtain the McGucken Operator *D_M*; read off its coefficients; take their ratio. The result is i*c* = *dx*₄/*dt*. □

**Theorem 6.14 (Constraint-containment of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in the McGucken Space; Theorem 5.4 of [66]).** The law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is contained in the McGucken Space *M_G* through its second component, the constraint function Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*.

*Proof.* Differentiating Φ_M along a curve (*t*, *x*₄(*t*)) gives *d*Φ_M/*dt* = *dx*₄/*dt* − i*c*. A curve lying in the constraint surface has Φ_M = 0 at every *t*, hence *d*Φ_M/*dt* = 0 along it, and therefore *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The extraction procedure is: project onto the second component to obtain Φ_M; differentiate with respect to *t* along a curve in the zero set; set the result to zero. □

**Corollary 6.15 (Two-fold containment; Corollary 5.5 of [66]).** The McGucken Space contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in two ways at once, through the McGucken Operator *D_M* (Theorem 6.13) and through Φ_M (Theorem 6.14), and both extractions give *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*.

*Proof.* Theorems 6.13 and 6.14 establish the two extractions independently, and each ends in *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. □

**Theorem 6.16 (Mutual Containment Theorem; Theorem 5.7 of [66]).** Each member of the source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) contains the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in full: (a) the McGucken Operator contains it as the ratio of its coefficients under tangency and normalization; (b) the McGucken Space contains it both through the McGucken Operator *D_M* as third component and through Φ_M as second component.

*Proof.* (a) is Theorem 6.12. (b) is Corollary 6.15. □

**Theorem 6.17 (Operator-to-Space generation procedure; Theorem 5.9 of [66]).** There is an explicit four-step procedure that produces the McGucken Space *M_G* from the McGucken Operator *D_M* alone.

*Proof (by construction).* The sole input is *D_M* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄. *Step 1 (carrier).* The McGucken Operator *D_M* differentiates with respect to *t* and *x*₄ and acts on functions of (*t*, **x**, *x*₄). The carrier is therefore the four-coordinate space *E*₄ of Definition 6.1 with parameter *t*. *Step 2 (kernel).* By Lemma 6.10 the solutions of *D_M* *Ψ* = 0 are the functions *F*(*x*₄ − i*ct*, **x**): they depend on (*t*, *x*₄) through the combination *u* = *x*₄ − i*ct* alone. *Step 3 (constraint).* The kernel consists of the functions constant on the level sets of *u*. [The kernel determines these level sets; among the functions of *u*, the normalization ∂₄Φ_M = 1 and Φ_M(0, 0) = 0 selects Φ_M = *u*.] The constraint function is Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, and the constraint surface is its zero set. *Step 4 (the McGucken Sphere).* On the constraint surface *x*₄ = i*ct*, so *dx*₄ = i*c* *dt* and *dx*₄² = −*c*²*dt*². Substituting into the four-coordinate Euclidean interval *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄² gives the Lorentzian interval *d***x**² − *c*²*dt*². Its vanishing, |*d***x**| = *c* *dt*, defines from each event *p* the set of points at distance *r* = *c*(*t* − *t*₀): the McGucken Sphere of Definition 6.4. Steps 1–4 give the carrier, the constraint function, the operator and the McGucken Sphere at every event, which together are the McGucken Space *M_G*. □

**Theorem 6.18 (Space-to-Operator generation procedure; Theorem 5.11 of [66]).** There is an explicit three-step procedure that produces the McGucken Operator *D_M* from the McGucken Space *M_G*, using only the carrier and the constraint function.

*Proof (by construction).* *Step 1 (differentiate the constraint).* Read Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct* from the McGucken Space *M_G*. By the proof of Theorem 6.14, a curve in the constraint surface satisfies *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. *Step 2 (chain rule).* For *Ψ*(*t*, *x*₄, **x**) along a curve satisfying *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, *dΨ*/*dt* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + (*dx*₄/*dt*) ∂₄*Ψ* = ∂ₜ*Ψ* + i*c* ∂₄*Ψ*. *Step 3 (identify the operator).* The right-hand side is the action of ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ = *D_M*. The procedure does not use the third component of the McGucken Space *M_G* as input; it produces the McGucken Operator *D_M* as output. □

**Theorem 6.19 (Mutual inverseness; Theorem 5.13 of [66]).** The procedure of Theorem 6.17 followed by the procedure of Theorem 6.18 returns the McGucken Operator *D_M*, and the procedure of Theorem 6.18 followed by the procedure of Theorem 6.17 returns the McGucken Space *M_G*.

*Proof.* *From the McGucken Space and back.* Start from the McGucken Space *M_G*. Theorem 6.18 gives the McGucken Operator *D_M*. Apply Theorem 6.17 to the McGucken Operator *D_M*: Step 1 recovers the carrier *E*₄ from the coordinates on which the McGucken Operator *D_M* acts, which is the carrier of the McGucken Space *M_G*; Steps 2 and 3 recover Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, which is the constraint function of the McGucken Space *M_G*; Step 4 constructs the McGucken Sphere at every event from the constraint, which is the assignment in the McGucken Space *M_G*. The composition returns the McGucken Space *M_G*. *From the McGucken Operator and back.* Start from the McGucken Operator *D_M*. Theorem 6.17 gives the McGucken Space *M_G* with constraint function Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*. Apply Theorem 6.18: Step 1 yields *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; Step 2 yields the chain-rule operator ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄; Step 3 identifies it as the McGucken Operator *D_M*. The composition returns the McGucken Operator *D_M*. □

**Theorem 6.20 (Reciprocal Generation Theorem; Theorem 5.14 of [66]).** Each member of the source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) generates the other: (a) the four-step procedure of Theorem 6.17 produces the McGucken Space *M_G* from the McGucken Operator *D_M*; (b) the three-step procedure of Theorem 6.18 produces the McGucken Operator *D_M* from the McGucken Space *M_G*; (c) the two procedures are mutually inverse.

*Proof.* (a), (b) and (c) are Theorems 6.17, 6.18 and 6.19. □

**Theorem 6.21 (Containment implies generation; Theorem 5.16 of [66]).** If *X* contains a relation *R* in the sense of Definition 6.7, then *X* generates whatever follows from *R* by a definite construction. For the source-pair, the Mutual Containment Theorem implies the Reciprocal Generation Theorem.

*Proof.* By Definition 6.7 there is a procedure extracting *R* from *X*. If *Y* follows from *R* by a definite construction, composing the extraction with that construction is a definite procedure producing *Y* from *X*, so *X* generates *Y* by Definition 6.8. Apply this to the source-pair. By Theorem 6.16, the McGucken Operator *D_M* contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and the McGucken Space *M_G* contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* produces the McGucken Space *M_G* by a definite construction (integration with *x*₄(0) = 0 to *x*₄ = i*ct*, the constraint Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct*, the carrier, and the McGucken Sphere by Step 4 of Theorem 6.17), and it produces the McGucken Operator *D_M* by the chain rule of Lemma 6.11(b). Hence the McGucken Operator *D_M* generates the McGucken Space *M_G* and the McGucken Space *M_G* generates the McGucken Operator *D_M*. □

**Theorem 6.22 (Generation implies containment; Theorem 5.17 of [66]).** If *X* generates *Y* in the sense of Definition 6.8, then *X* contains the defining data of *Y*. For the source-pair, the Reciprocal Generation Theorem implies the Mutual Containment Theorem.

*Proof.* By Definition 6.8 there is a definite procedure producing *Y* from *X*; that procedure extracts *Y* from *X*, which is containment in the sense of Definition 6.7. Apply this to the source-pair. The procedure of Theorem 6.17 produces from the McGucken Operator *D_M* the function Φ_M (Steps 2 and 3), and *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* is extracted from Φ_M by Theorem 6.14; so the McGucken Operator *D_M* contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The procedure of Theorem 6.18 produces *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* from Φ_M in its first step; so the McGucken Space *M_G* contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* through Φ_M, and through the McGucken Operator *D_M* by Theorem 6.13. □

**Theorem 6.23 (Containment-Generation Equivalence; Theorem 5.18 of [66]).** For the source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*), the Mutual Containment Theorem and the Reciprocal Generation Theorem are equivalent: each member of the pair contains *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* in full if and only if each member generates the other.

*Proof.* The Mutual Containment Theorem implies the Reciprocal Generation Theorem by Theorem 6.21. The Reciprocal Generation Theorem implies the Mutual Containment Theorem by Theorem 6.22. □

**Corollary 6.24 (The pair is a single object; Corollary 5.19 of [66]).** The source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) is the law *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* written in two notations: the geometric notation the McGucken Space *M_G*, with its carrier, constraint and McGucken Sphere, and the operator notation *D_M* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄.

*Proof.* By Theorem 6.16 the law is present in both members. By Theorem 6.20 each member determines the other through mutually inverse procedures. A pair in which each member holds the same law and each is recovered from the other carries one piece of data, *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. □

## **The Double Completion of the Erlangen Programme.**

The most striking feature of the completion is that it occurs in two derivationally independent ways at once. There are two routes from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* to the Klein pair, and each route completes the Erlangen Programme in its own language [67, 68]. The theorem is the Erlangen Double-Completion (Theorem 21 of [67]; Theorems 1–3 of [68]; Theorem 7.1 of [74]), with Route 1 proved as Theorem 6 of [67], Route 2 as Theorem 11 of [67], and the independence of the two routes as Theorem 14 of [67]: Route 1 (group-theoretic, Klein-internal) supplies the physical generator that selects the relativistic Klein pair (ISO(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) from within Klein’s group-invariant architecture; Route 2 (category-theoretic, Klein-deepening) replaces Klein’s primitive group-space pair (*G*, *X*) with the co-generated source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) and replaces the Klein category with McG₆ through the six-object source-tuple and its three adjunctions. Both routes are rooted in the same axiom (in the mathematical reading) and the same physical principle (in the physical reading); their independence is the independence of the two languages, not of two postulates.

**Route 1: The Group-Theoretic Route.** The first route works in the language Klein himself used. The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, the statement that *x*₄ expands at rate *c* from every event, is preserved by exactly the transformations that intermix (*t*, **x**) while leaving *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* invariant. That invariance group is the Lorentz/Poincaré group; it is derived, not posited. The stabilizer of any chosen event is the Lorentz subgroup; the homogeneous space *G*/*H* is Minkowski space; the Klein pair (Iso(1,3), SO(1,3)) emerges as the unique pair that leaves the source law invariant [67]. In Klein’s own language, then, the axiom *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* singles out the geometry of relativity by the symmetry-criterion of 1872. The theorems are these [59]. Under the labelled hypotheses A1–A5 of [59] (a connected, oriented, smooth event four-manifold; local orthonormal coordinates in which the Principle reads *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; the Euclidean four-coordinate inner product *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄²; event homogeneity; the +i*c* orientation), substituting *dx*₄ = i*c* *dt* gives the form η = *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*² of signature (3,1) (Lemma 1 of [59]), and the connected component of its real-linear isometry group is the proper orthochronous Lorentz group SO⁺(1,3) (Theorem 24 of [59]). With flat completeness added (Hypothesis A6 of [59]), the connected group of affine isometries is the Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3) = **R**^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3) (Theorem 27 of [59]).

**Route 2: The Category-Theoretic Route.** The second route works in the language of Mac Lane and Eilenberg. The McGucken Principle, viewed as the simultaneous generator of the source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*), defines the category **McG** directly. The Klein pairs proved in this paper — Lorentz/Poincaré (Theorem 6.30), unitary (Theorem 6.33), symplectic (Theorem 6.34), Euclidean (Theorem 6.36), Galilean (Theorem 6.37), spin (Theorem 6.38) and conformal (Theorem 6.39) — are obtained from the source-pair, and the morphisms between their arenas are the correspondences (Wick rotation, geometric quantization, gauge covariantization) that physicists construct by hand in the standard formulation [68]. The source law plays the role of the universal morphism: every arrow in **McG** is an instance of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* acting on its co-generated source-pair.

**Why the Two Routes Agree.** The two routes are not two competing derivations whose agreement requires explanation; they are two faithful translations of the same source law into two languages. The group route fixes the symmetry of a single physical region by isolating the invariance group of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The category route fixes the relations *between* regions by exhibiting **McG** as the category in which those regions appear as objects and those relations as morphisms. Yet both descend, step by step, from the same one-line law: in Route 1 the law is the invariance criterion; in Route 2 the law is the source of objects and morphisms. They agree on the recovered geometry because they cannot disagree — the recovered geometry is, in both cases, the geometry of *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* itself.

Together, the two routes constitute a *double completion*: the Erlangen Programme is finished simultaneously in group theory and in category theory, by the same axiom [67, 68]. This is not a coincidence between independent results but a feature of the source law — that one equation is rich enough to fix the geometry under both criteria, the symmetry-classification of 1872 and the categorical organization of 1945.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of Klein’s Erlangen Programme: The Two Routes to the Klein Pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)), with Proofs.**

**McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. For Klein’s programme the Principle predicts the group and the subgroup that Klein’s rule takes as given: the interval generated by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* has the Poincaré group as its invariance group and the Lorentz group as the stabilizer of an event. The statements and proofs below are those of [67] (Lemmas 4 and 5, Theorems 6, 9, 11 and 14) and [66] (Theorem 7.18).

**Lemma 6.25 (Lorentzian signature from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; Lemma 4 of [67]).** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* generates the Lorentzian line element *ds*² = *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*² of signature (−, +, +, +).

*Proof.* From *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, *dx*₄ = i*c* *dt* along any integral curve. Squaring, *dx*₄² = (i*c*)² *dt*² = −*c*²*dt*². The four-coordinate carrier *E*₄ has line element *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄². Substituting gives *ds*² = *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*². The minus sign is generated by i² = −1 and the factor *c*² by the rate in the Principle. (§9.1 gives the same computation as the pullback of the quadratic form along the embedding of the constraint surface.) □

**Lemma 6.26 (Invariance group of the interval; Lemma 5 of [67]).** The group of invertible affine transformations of ℝ⁴ with coordinates *x*^μ = (*ct*, *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃) preserving the line element *ds*² = *η*_μν *dx*^μ *dx*^ν, *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), is the group ISO(1,3) of maps *x* ↦ *Λx* + *a* with *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η* and *a* ∈ ℝ⁴. Its connected component of the identity is the Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3) = ℝ^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3). The stabilizer of an event in ISO⁺(1,3) is SO⁺(1,3), and ℝ^(1,3) ≅ ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3).

*Proof.* Under an affine map *x* ↦ *Λx* + *a* the differentials transform as *dx* ↦ *Λ* *dx*, so *ds*² ↦ *dx*ᵀ *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* *dx*. This equals *dx*ᵀ *η* *dx* for all *dx* exactly when *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η*, because a symmetric matrix is determined by its quadratic form. The matrices with *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η* form the group O(1,3); with the translations they form ISO(1,3). Taking determinants gives (det *Λ*)² = 1, and the 00 component of *Λ*ᵀ*ηΛ* = *η* gives (*Λ*⁰₀)² = 1 + Σ_i (*Λ*ⁱ₀)² ≥ 1; the two signs of det *Λ* and of *Λ*⁰₀ separate O(1,3) into four pieces, and the piece with det *Λ* = 1 and *Λ*⁰₀ ≥ 1, which contains the identity, is SO⁺(1,3). This piece is connected: for *Λ* in it, the boost *B* that carries the unit time vector *e*₀ to *Λe*₀ lies on a one-parameter subgroup through the identity, and *B*⁻¹*Λ* fixes *e*₀ and has determinant 1, hence is a rotation of the orthogonal three-space, an element of the connected group SO(3). The translations ℝ⁴ are connected, so the identity component of ISO(1,3) is ℝ^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3). An element *x* ↦ *Λx* + *a* fixes the event *x* = 0 exactly when *a* = 0, so the stabilizer of that event is SO⁺(1,3), and the stabilizer of any other event is conjugate to it by a translation. The translations carry *x* = 0 to every event, so the action is transitive, and the map sending the coset of an element to the image of *x* = 0 identifies ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3) with ℝ^(1,3). □

**Lemma 6.27 (The isometries of the interval are affine).** Let *X* = *X*^μ ∂_μ be a vector field on ℝ⁴ whose flow preserves *η*_μν *dx*^μ *dx*^ν. Then *X*_μ = *a*_μ + *ω*_μν *x*^ν with *a*_μ constant and *ω*_μν = −*ω*_νμ constant. The flows of these fields are the translations and the one-parameter subgroups of SO⁺(1,3).

*Proof.* With *X*_μ = *η*_μν *X*^ν, preservation of the interval by the flow is the Killing equation ∂_μ *X*_ν + ∂_ν *X*_μ = 0. Differentiating, ∂_ρ ∂_μ *X*_ν + ∂_ρ ∂_ν *X*_μ = 0. Write *T*_ρμν = ∂_ρ ∂_μ *X*_ν, which is symmetric in its first two indices; the equation states *T*_ρμν = −*T*_ρνμ. Then *T*_ρμν = −*T*_ρνμ = −*T*_νρμ = *T*_νμρ = *T*_μνρ = −*T*_μρν = −*T*_ρμν, using the two properties alternately, so *T*_ρμν = 0. Hence each *X*_ν is a polynomial of degree at most one, *X*_μ = *a*_μ + *ω*_μν *x*^ν, and the Killing equation gives *ω*_μν + *ω*_νμ = 0. The flow of the constant field *a* is translation by multiples of *a*. The flow of *ω*^μ_ν *x*^ν is *x* ↦ exp(*sω*) *x*, and (*ηω*)ᵀ = −*ηω* gives exp(*sω*)ᵀ *η* exp(*sω*) = *η*, with exp(*sω*) connected to the identity through *s* → 0, so exp(*sω*) ∈ SO⁺(1,3). □

**Theorem 6.28 (Route 1, symmetry route; Theorem 6 of [67]).** Let the McGucken Principle be the primitive physical relation *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. Then the Klein pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) of relativistic spacetime is derived from the McGucken Principle.

*Proof.* By Lemma 6.25, the McGucken Principle generates the Lorentzian line element. By Lemma 6.26, the invariance group of this line element is ISO(1,3), with identity component the Poincaré group ISO⁺(1,3), the stabilizer of an event is SO⁺(1,3), and ℝ^(1,3) ≅ ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3). Each step is forced by the Principle: the signature by i² = −1, the rate by the factor *c*, the invariance group by the resulting line element, the stabilizer by the action at a fixed event. □

**Theorem 6.29 (Source-pair co-generation along the flow; Theorem 9 of [67]).** (i) The level sets of Φ_M = *x*₄ − i*ct* are the integral curves of the vector field *V* = ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ in the (*t*, *x*₄)-plane. (ii) The directional derivative along *V* is the McGucken Operator *D_M*. (iii) Both arise from the single relation *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*, as its integral form and its differential form.

*Proof.* (i) The integral curves of *V* satisfy *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* by Lemma 6.11(a). Integrating from a point (*t*₀, *x*₄⁰) gives *x*₄(*t*) − *x*₄⁰ = i*c*(*t* − *t*₀), that is, Φ_M(*t*, *x*₄) = Φ_M(*t*₀, *x*₄⁰): each integral curve is a level set of Φ_M, and each level set is such a curve. (ii) The directional derivative along *V* is ∂ₜ + i*c* ∂₄ = *D_M*. (iii) *V* is the vector field with unit *t*-component whose integral curves satisfy *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*; the level sets of Φ_M and the McGucken Operator *D_M* are both read from *V*. □

**Theorem 6.30 (Route 2, source-pair route; Theorem 11 of [67] and Theorem 7.18 of [66]).** The Klein pair (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)) is obtained from the source-pair (McGucken Space *M_G*, McGucken Operator *D_M*) in four steps, each determined by *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*.

*Proof.* *Step 1, from the law to the constraint surface.* *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* with *x*₄(0) = 0 integrates to *x*₄ = i*ct*, the zero set of Φ_M (Theorem 6.29(i)). *Step 2, from the constraint surface to the Lorentzian metric.* Parametrize the constraint surface, taken with the three spatial coordinates, by (*t*, **x**) ↦ (**x**, i*ct*). The pullback of *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄² along this parametrization is *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² − *c*²*dt*² by Lemma 6.25. *Step 3, from the metric to the isometry group.* By Lemma 6.27 every one-parameter group of isometries of this metric consists of the affine maps of Lemma 6.26, and by Lemma 6.26 the connected group they generate is ISO⁺(1,3) = ℝ^(1,3) ⋊ SO⁺(1,3). The orientation +i*c* of the Principle is the time orientation preserved by SO⁺(1,3). *Step 4, from the group to the Klein pair.* Choose the event *p*₀ at the origin of the expansion. By Lemma 6.26 its stabilizer is SO⁺(1,3) and ISO⁺(1,3)/SO⁺(1,3) ≅ ℝ^(1,3). The chain is *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* → Φ_M → Lorentzian metric → ISO⁺(1,3) → (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)). In this route the source-pair is constructed before any group is introduced, and the Klein pair is read from the metric that the source-pair carries. □

**Theorem 6.31 (Independence of the two routes; Theorem 14 of [67]).** (i) Route 1 (Theorem 6.28) has the Klein pair as its object and constructs no source-pair. (ii) Route 2 (Theorem 6.30) has the source-pair as its object and reaches the Klein pair after the source-pair is constructed. (iii) The two routes share the input *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and the endpoint (ISO⁺(1,3), SO⁺(1,3)).

*Proof.* (i) The proof of Theorem 6.28 uses Lemmas 6.25 and 6.26 only: it derives a metric and then that metric’s invariance group. Neither the McGucken Space *M_G* nor the McGucken Operator *D_M* appears in it. (ii) The proof of Theorem 6.30 begins with Theorem 6.29, which constructs Φ_M and the McGucken Operator *D_M* from the flow before any group action is introduced, and it reaches the group in Step 3. (iii) Both proofs start from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* and end at the same pair; both use Lemma 6.25 for the metric, which is where the two routes meet. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Klein’s rule.** Klein’s 1872 rule takes a group *G* and a subgroup *H* as given and studies the invariants of *G* on *G*/*H*. Theorems 6.28 and 6.30 derive the pair for relativistic spacetime, *G* = ISO⁺(1,3) and *H* = SO⁺(1,3), from *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c*. The scope is stated in Theorem 4 of [68]: “The paper proves that the physical Lorentzian Klein pair arises from the McGucken Principle along two independent routes.”

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Unitary and Symplectic Klein Pairs of Mechanics, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. Theorems 6.28 and 6.30 obtain from it the Klein pair of spacetime as the group that preserves the interval. The same Principle gives two further quantities, and it predicts a Klein pair for each: the total of the Born density, ∫ |*f*|² *d*³*x*, conserved by the expansion of *x*₄ (Theorem 8.4.1), whose group is the unitary group acting on the space of states (Theorem 6.33); and the commutator i*ℏ* of position and momentum (Theorem 8.3.5), whose group is the affine symplectic group acting on phase space (Theorem 6.34).

**Definition 6.32 (Klein pair).** A Klein pair (*G*, *H*) with space *X* consists of a group *G* acting on a set *X* so that every point of *X* is carried to every other by some element of *G*, and the subgroup *H* of the elements that fix a chosen point *x*₀. The map that sends *g* to *gx*₀ takes the same value on *g* and *g*′ exactly when *g*⁻¹*g*′ lies in *H*, so it identifies *X* with the set of cosets *gH*. In Theorem 6.28, *X* is spacetime, *G* = ISO⁺(1,3) and *H* = SO⁺(1,3).

**Theorem 6.33 (The unitary Klein pair of the space of states).** Let ℋ be the Hilbert space of Theorem 8.4.3 with the inner product ⟨*f*|*g*⟩ of Theorem 8.4.2 and *N*(*f*) = ⟨*f*|*f*⟩. A state is a ray [*φ*], the set of the vectors *λφ* with |*λ*| = 1, for a vector *φ* with *N*(*φ*) = 1; by Proposition 9.3.2 these are the wavefunctions of one emission referred to the different origins of *x*₄. Let *X* be the set of states and let U(ℋ) be the set of linear maps *W* of ℋ onto itself with ⟨*Wf*|*Wg*⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩ for all *f*, *g*. (i) A linear map of ℋ onto itself that preserves *N* preserves the inner product; so U(ℋ) is the group of the linear bijections that preserve the conserved total *N*. (ii) U(ℋ) acts on *X* by [*φ*] → [*Wφ*], and the number |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|², which depends on the two states alone, is unchanged by the action. (iii) For any two states there is an element of U(ℋ) carrying the first to the second. (iv) Fix a state [*φ*₀] and let *K* be the set of the vectors *g* with ⟨*φ*₀|*g*⟩ = 0. The elements of U(ℋ) that fix [*φ*₀] are the maps *W*(*aφ*₀ + *g*) = *λaφ*₀ + *W*′*g*, with |*λ*| = 1 and *W*′ a unitary map of *K* onto itself; this subgroup is U(1) × U(*K*). Hence (U(ℋ), U(1) × U(*K*)) is a Klein pair with space *X*. (v) The evolutions of Theorem 8.4.3(i) belong to U(ℋ). The conjugation *f* → *f*∗ carries states to states and leaves |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|² unchanged, and it does not belong to U(ℋ).

*Proof.* (i) For a form that is conjugate-linear in the first argument and linear in the second, 4⟨*f*|*g*⟩ = *N*(*f* + *g*) − *N*(*f* − *g*) − i*N*(*f* + i*g*) + i*N*(*f* − i*g*): the first two terms give 4 Re⟨*f*|*g*⟩, and *N*(*f* + i*g*) − *N*(*f* − i*g*) = 4 Re(i⟨*f*|*g*⟩) = −4 Im⟨*f*|*g*⟩. A linear map *W* with *N*(*Wf*) = *N*(*f*) for all *f* preserves each of the four terms, since *W*(*f* ± *g*) = *Wf* ± *Wg* and *W*(*f* ± i*g*) = *Wf* ± i*Wg*. The composition of two such maps and the inverse of one are such maps. (ii) If *N*(*φ*) = 1 then *N*(*Wφ*) = 1, and *W*(*λφ*) = *λWφ*, so the ray of *φ* is carried into the ray of *Wφ*. Replacing *φ* by *λφ* and *ψ* by *λ*′*ψ* multiplies ⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩ by a number of modulus 1, and ⟨*Wφ*|*Wψ*⟩ = ⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩.

(iii) Let *N*(*φ*) = *N*(*ψ*) = 1. Choose *λ* with |*λ*| = 1 so that *χ* = *λψ*, a vector of the ray of *ψ*, has ⟨*φ*|*χ*⟩ = *s* real and non-negative. If *χ* = *φ*, the identity map serves. Otherwise *N*(*φ* − *χ*) = 2 − 2*s* > 0; put *w* = (*φ* − *χ*)/(2 − 2*s*)^(1/2), so that *N*(*w*) = 1, and *Wf* = *f* − 2⟨*w*|*f*⟩*w*. This map is linear, and *W*(*Wf*) = *f* − 4⟨*w*|*f*⟩*w* + 4⟨*w*|*f*⟩⟨*w*|*w*⟩*w* = *f*, so it is a bijection. It preserves the inner product: ⟨*Wf*|*Wg*⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩ − 2⟨*f*|*w*⟩⟨*w*|*g*⟩ − 2⟨*f*|*w*⟩⟨*w*|*g*⟩ + 4⟨*f*|*w*⟩⟨*w*|*g*⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩. And ⟨*w*|*φ*⟩ = (1 − *s*)/(2 − 2*s*)^(1/2), so 2⟨*w*|*φ*⟩*w* = 2(1 − *s*)(*φ* − *χ*)/(2 − 2*s*) = *φ* − *χ* and *Wφ* = *χ*. (iv) Let *W* be in U(ℋ) with *Wφ*₀ = *λφ*₀. If ⟨*φ*₀|*g*⟩ = 0 then ⟨*λφ*₀|*Wg*⟩ = ⟨*Wφ*₀|*Wg*⟩ = 0, so *W* maps *K* into *K*; the inverse of *W* fixes the same ray, so it maps *K* into *K* too, and *W* maps *K* onto *K*, preserving the inner product. Every vector is *aφ*₀ + *g* with *a* = ⟨*φ*₀|*f*⟩ and *g* in *K*, in exactly one way, which gives the stated form. Conversely each map of that form is linear, onto, and preserves *N*, since *N*(*aφ*₀ + *g*) = |*a*|² + *N*(*g*). The correspondence of *W* with the pair (*λ*, *W*′) respects composition. With (iii) and Definition 6.32 this is the Klein pair. (v) The first statement is Theorem 8.4.3(i). For the conjugation, ⟨*f*∗|*g*∗⟩ = ⟨*f*|*g*⟩∗, which has the same modulus; but (i*f*)∗ = −i*f*∗, so the conjugation is not a linear map. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem 6.33.** The invariant of this geometry is |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|², the Born probability of §9.3, as the interval is the invariant of the geometry of Theorem 6.28. The group is fixed by the Principle through part (i): it is the group of the linear maps that preserve the total conserved by the expansion of *x*₄. The linearity is with respect to the complex numbers, whose unit i is the i of *x*₄ = i*ct*; the conjugation of part (v) is the conjugate expansion *x*₄∗ = −i*ct* of Definition 9.3.1, and it lies outside the group. Wigner’s theorem [87], that every bijection of the states preserving |⟨*φ*|*ψ*⟩|² comes from a unitary map or from a unitary map followed by the conjugation, is an external theorem and is not used.

**Theorem 6.34 (The symplectic Klein pair of phase space).** Let *q*₁, *q*₂, *q*₃ and *p*₁, *p*₂, *p*₃ be the operators of position and momentum of Theorem 8.3.5 on the Schwartz functions, and write *r* for the column of these six operators in this order. Let *J* be the 6 × 6 matrix with the blocks 0, 1 in the first row and −1, 0 in the second, each block 3 × 3, and for columns *z*, *z*′ of six real numbers let *ω*(*z*, *z*′) = *z*ᵀ*Jz*′. (i) The canonical relations are *r*_m*r*_l − *r*_l*r*_m = i*ℏ* *J*_ml for *m*, *l* = 1, …, 6. For *z* = (*a*, *b*) put *L*(*z*) = Σ_j (*a*_j*q*_j + *b*_j*p*_j); then *L*(*z*)*L*(*z*′) − *L*(*z*′)*L*(*z*) = i*ℏ* *ω*(*z*, *z*′). (ii) Let *S* be a real 6 × 6 matrix and *ζ* a column of six real numbers, and let *r*′ = *Sr* + *ζ*, the six operators *r*′_m = Σ_l *S*_ml*r*_l + *ζ*_m. The operators *r*′ satisfy the canonical relations if and only if *SJS*ᵀ = *J*. (iii) *SJS*ᵀ = *J* if and only if *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J*, that is, *ω*(*Sz*, *Sz*′) = *ω*(*z*, *z*′) for all *z*, *z*′. The matrices with this property form a group, the real symplectic group Sp(6, ℝ). (iv) The maps *z* → *Sz* + *ζ* of the phase space ℝ⁶ of the values of position and momentum, with *S* in Sp(6, ℝ), form a group *G* under composition, and carrying out two transformations of (ii) in succession corresponds to composing the two maps. *G* carries every point of ℝ⁶ to every other, and the elements of *G* fixing the origin are those with *ζ* = 0. Hence (*G*, Sp(6, ℝ)) is a Klein pair with space ℝ⁶.

*Proof.* (i) By Theorem 8.3.5(ii), *q*_j*p*_k − *p*_k*q*_j = i*ℏ* δ_jk. Two multiplications by coordinates commute, and two partial derivatives of a Schwartz function commute, so *q*_j*q*_k − *q*_k*q*_j = 0 and *p*_j*p*_k − *p*_k*p*_j = 0. In the order of the column *r* these are the stated relations: *J* has the entry 1 in the row of *q*_j and the column of *p*_j, the entry −1 in the row of *p*_j and the column of *q*_j, and zeros elsewhere. Since *L*(*z*) = Σ_m *z*_m*r*_m, the commutator of *L*(*z*) and *L*(*z*′) is Σ_m Σ_l *z*_m*z*′_l i*ℏ* *J*_ml = i*ℏ* *z*ᵀ*Jz*′. (ii) Multiples of the identity commute with every operator, so the commutator of *r*′_m and *r*′_n is Σ_l Σ_k *S*_ml*S*_nk(*r*_l*r*_k − *r*_k*r*_l) = i*ℏ* Σ_l Σ_k *S*_ml*J*_lk*S*_nk = i*ℏ*(*SJS*ᵀ)_mn. The canonical relations for *r*′ state that this equals i*ℏ* *J*_mn for all *m*, *n*. (iii) *J*² = −1, so *J* is invertible, and *SJS*ᵀ = *J* shows that the determinant of *S* is not zero. From *SJS*ᵀ = *J*, *JS*ᵀ = *S*⁻¹*J*, hence *S*ᵀ = *J*⁻¹*S*⁻¹*J* and *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J*⁻¹*S*⁻¹*J*²*S* = −*J*⁻¹ = *J*. The converse is the same computation applied to *S*ᵀ. Since *ω*(*Sz*, *Sz*′) = *z*ᵀ*S*ᵀ*JSz*′, the condition *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J* is the invariance of *ω*. If *S* and *T* satisfy it, so do *ST*, because (*ST*)ᵀ*J*(*ST*) = *T*ᵀ(*S*ᵀ*JS*)*T* = *J*, and *S*⁻¹, because multiplying *S*ᵀ*JS* = *J* by the inverses on both sides gives *J* = (*S*⁻¹)ᵀ*JS*⁻¹. (iv) The composition of *z* → *Sz* + *ζ* after *z* → *Tz* + *ξ* is *z* → *STz* + *Sξ* + *ζ*, and the inverse of *z* → *Sz* + *ζ* is *z* → *S*⁻¹*z* − *S*⁻¹*ζ*; by (iii) both have their matrix in Sp(6, ℝ). Applying (ii) with (*T*, *ξ*) and then with (*S*, *ζ*) to the result gives *S*(*Tr* + *ξ*) + *ζ* = *STr* + *Sξ* + *ζ*. The map *z* → *z* + (*z*₂ − *z*₁) carries *z*₁ to *z*₂, and *S*·0 + *ζ* = 0 exactly when *ζ* = 0. With Definition 6.32 this is the Klein pair. □

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* reading of Theorem 6.34.** The form *ω* is the commutator of Theorem 8.3.5 divided by i*ℏ*: the i is the i of *x*₄ = i*ct* and ℏ is the action of one wavelength of the McGucken Sphere divided by 2π. Part (ii) shows that the group of this geometry is fixed by that commutator, as the group of Theorem 6.28 is fixed by the interval: the affine symplectic group, with the translations of phase space in the place of the translations of spacetime and Sp(6, ℝ) in the place of SO⁺(1,3). Its elements are the linear canonical transformations of Hamilton’s mechanics. The theorem is stated for one body; for *n* coordinates the same proof gives Sp(2*n*, ℝ). The construction of unitary operators on ℋ that carry out these transformations is not part of the theorem.

**The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* Reading of the Euclidean, Galilean, Spin and Conformal Klein Pairs, with Proofs.** **McGucken Principle anchor.** The McGucken Principle *dx*₄/*dt* = i*c* states that the fourth dimension is expanding at the velocity of light in a spherically symmetric manner from every spacetime event, as a McGucken Sphere whose wavelength sets the action *h*. From the interval *dx*₁² + *dx*₂² + *dx*₃² + *dx*₄² that the Principle gives (§9.1) and the group of Theorem 6.28, it predicts four further Klein pairs: the Euclidean pair of the three dimensions *x*₁, *x*₂, *x*₃ at one value of *t* (Theorem 6.36); the Galilean pair, reached when the rate *c* is taken large compared with every other velocity (Theorem 6.37); the spin pair, whose group acts on the two-component amplitudes of Theorem 8.3.7 and covers the group of Theorem 6.28 twice (Theorem 6.38); and the conformal pair, whose invariant is the null cone *ds*² = 0 traced by the McGucken Spheres of an event (Theorem 6.39). Throughout, *x* = (*x*⁰, **x**) with *x*⁰ = *ct*, *η* = diag(−1, 1, 1, 1), and *η*(*x*, *y*) = *η*_μν*x*^μ*y*^ν.

**Lemma 6.35 (The group SO⁺(1,3) by its conditions).** Let 𝒟₊ be the set of the real 4 × 4 matrices Λ with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*, Λ⁰₀ ≥ 1 and det Λ = 1. (i) Every rotation of ℝ³ is a product of rotations about the coordinate axes. (ii) Every Λ in 𝒟₊ is a product of a rotation, a boost along the first axis (Theorem 8.1.7) and a rotation. (iii) 𝒟₊ is the connected component of the identity among the matrices with Λᵀ*η*Λ = *η*; it is the group SO⁺(1,3) of Lemma 6.26.

*Proof.* (i) Let ρ be a rotation and **v** = ρ**e**₃, with **e**₃ the unit vector of the third axis. By the polar angles of **v** there are a rotation *Y* about the second axis and a rotation *Z* about the third axis with *ZY***e**₃ = **v**.
